371 — Linear Programming / Simplex Solver from Scratch

Project Number: 371

Project Name: Linear Programming / Simplex Solver from Scratch

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A from-scratch, production-quality linear-programming solver in Rust implementing the full simplex method lineage: dense-tableau reference engine, revised simplex with LU-factorized basis, two-phase feasibility, bounded-variable simplex, dual simplex for warm-start reoptimization, presolve/scaling, and post-optimal sensitivity analysis. The solver reads LP problems in MPS or LP file format, converts to standard form, solves, and returns a certified result (optimal/infeasible/unbounded) with a verifiable certificate.

1.2 System Boundaries

In scope Out of scope
Primal simplex (dense tableau + revised) Interior-point (IPM) method — stretch goal only
Dual simplex + warm start Mixed-integer programming (branch-and-bound) — stretch goal
Two-phase feasibility + Big-M (documented alternative) Quadratic programming
Bounded-variable simplex (native lb/ub) Nonlinear constraints
LU factorization + Bartels-Golub/Forrest-Tomlin updates Network simplex
Bland/Dantzig/Devex pricing Parallel/distributed solving
Presolve (singleton/redundancy/bound tightening) + scaling GPU acceleration
Sensitivity analysis (ranging, shadow prices, reduced costs) SaaS API / server mode
MPS + LP format parsers; CLI Automatic differentiation
KKT certificate verification on every optimal Stochastic/robust LP
Netlib LP conformance suite Commercial solver interfacing

1.3 Non-Goals

  • Not a general mathematical programming system; LP only in Phase 0–14.
  • No Python/Julia/C FFI bindings in core phases (may be added post-Phase 14).
  • No GUI or web frontend.
  • No NUMA-aware or multi-threaded factorization.
  • No warm-start from a third-party solver's basis format.

1.4 Deployment Model

  • Rust binary + library crate workspace; usable as a CLI tool or embedded as a library.
  • Single-threaded; deterministic pivot sequence for reproducibility.
  • Target: developer workstations, CI pipelines, embedded analysis tools, portfolio integration with projects #206/#313 (query optimizer cost model) and #372 (SVM as QP).
  • No network stack; no persistent state beyond a basis file written on demand.

1.5 Target Users

User class Interface Primary use
Application developer Library API (lp-model, lp-simplex, lp-sensitivity) Embed solver in optimization pipeline
Analyst / researcher lp-cli solve, lp-cli sensitivity Solve LP files, inspect shadow prices
Systems/compiler engineer lp-cli convert, lp-cli check Convert formats, verify KKT certificates
Student / learner Dense-tableau path + documentation Study simplex step-by-step

1.6 Phase 0 Deliverables — Locked Crate Inventory

The following crates form the workspace; all are present as stubs from Phase 1:

  1. lp-core — VarId, ConstraintId, Sense, Relation, Bound, Status, VarStatus; no I/O, no solving
  2. lp-model — LpProblem, SparseMatrix (CSC), StandardForm; standard-form conversion; builder + validation
  3. lp-tableau — dense (m × n) tableau; pivot; entering/leaving selection; optimality and ratio test
  4. lp-simplex — primal simplex driver; Phase I (two-phase); Phase II; optimality and unboundedness detection
  5. lp-pricing — PivotRule; Dantzig, Bland (anti-cycling), Devex/steepest-edge approximation
  6. lp-revised — revised simplex; basis representation; FTRAN/BTRAN; reduced-cost pricing without full tableau
  7. lp-lu — sparse LU of the basis matrix; partial pivoting; Forrest-Tomlin eta updates; refactorization trigger
  8. lp-bounds — bounded-variable simplex; VarStatus at lower/upper; bound-flip ratio test; range reduction
  9. lp-dual — dual simplex driver; dual-feasible pivoting; warm-start from saved Basis; bounded-dual ratio test
  10. lp-presolve — presolve passes (empty/singleton rows/cols, bound tightening, redundancy); geometric/Curtis-Reid scaling; postsolve map
  11. lp-sensitivity — Sensitivity; cost-coefficient ranging; RHS ranging; shadow prices; reduced-cost ranges
  12. lp-parse — MPS + LP file parsers; LpProblem builder from parsed data; solution writer
  13. lp-cli — solve | sensitivity | convert | check subcommands; orchestrates presolve→solve→postsolve→report

1.7 Phase Unlock Map

Phase Unlocks
1–2 Problem model, standard-form conversion

1.8 Estimated Timeline

Scope Duration
Phase 0 (crate stubs, core types, dense tableau) 6–14 weeks
Full Phase 1–14 6–12 months

1.9 Language Rationale — Rust (locked)

A simplex solver is numerically delicate dense/sparse linear algebra: pivoting, an LU factorization of the basis that must be refactorized when it drifts, tolerance-based feasibility/optimality tests, and ratio tests where one wrong sign loops forever. Rust gives tight control over f64 buffers (cache-friendly column-major basis storage, no hidden allocation in the pivot loop), exhaustive enums for variable status (Basic / NonbasicLower / NonbasicUpper / Free) and solve status, and slice-based numeric kernels with bounds-safety. The model, tableau and revised simplex, LU factorization + updates, presolve, dual simplex, and sensitivity analysis are all Rust; an interior-point method and integer branch-and-bound are explicit stretch goals, not Phase 0.

1.10 Prerequisites

Domain Required knowledge
Linear algebra Matrix rank, triangular systems, Gaussian elimination, LU factorization, condition number
LP theory Feasible region, vertex = BFS, optimality conditions (KKT, duality), Farkas' lemma
Numerical analysis Floating-point arithmetic, cancellation, pivot stability, partial pivoting, residual norms
Sparse data structures CSC/CSR formats, fill-in during factorization, eta-file representation
Rust Workspace crates, Vec<f64> buffer ownership, trait objects, iterator adapters, #[cfg(test)]
Algorithms Big-O complexity of simplex phases, worst-case vs. empirical behavior

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Taught

Topic What the builder learns
Simplex pivot mechanics How entering/leaving variable selection determines correctness and speed
LU basis factorization Why the basis is not stored explicitly; how FTRAN/BTRAN replace tableau row operations
Numerical hygiene Pivot tolerance, refactorization triggers, condition estimation without an eigenvalue solver
Anti-cycling proofs Why Bland's rule is correct; how degeneracy causes loops; lexicographic proof sketch
Duality How dual variables emerge from the basis; strong duality as a termination certificate
Presolve correctness How reductions must compose and invert; why postsolve order matters
Warm-start engineering Why a saved basis is a first-class type; how dual simplex reoptimizes in O(pivots) not O(solve)
Sensitivity analysis How ranging intervals are computed from the basis inverse; shadow price interpretation
Certificate generation Primal ray construction for unbounded; Farkas ray for infeasible
Format parsing MPS fixed/free format edge cases; LP format ambiguities

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • Simplex method (Dantzig, 1947) is one of the most impactful algorithms in 20th-century applied mathematics; still the dominant LP algorithm in practice despite IPM existence.
  • Every commercial LP solver (CPLEX, Gurobi, GLPK, HiGHS, CLP) has a simplex engine at its core; understanding internals separates users from implementers.
  • The revised simplex + LU-update architecture (Bartels-Golub 1969, Forrest-Tomlin 1972) is a case study in numerical stability engineering: a naive implementation diverges; the factorized one does not.
  • Duality theory is the conceptual bridge to Lagrangian relaxation, SVM (quadratic dual), and pricing in operations research.
  • Two-phase method and Big-M illustrate how a feasibility problem is reformulated as an optimization — the same trick appears in SAT (project #277) and constraint propagation.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Numerical discipline: tolerances as named constants; never == 0.0; pivot stability checks.
  • Invariant-driven design: I1–I10 are checked in debug_assert! throughout; the solver is self-auditing.
  • Layered abstraction: dense tableau as oracle, revised simplex as production; same external contract, different internals — classic layered-test architecture.
  • Certified computation: every result has a machine-checkable witness; verification is separate from generation.
  • Warm-start API design: Basis as a serializable first-class type; re-entry points that skip Phase I.
  • Format engineering: parsing MPS (fixed-width, legacy, quirky) without a combinator library.
  • Benchmarking under degeneracy: degenerate LPs are common; measuring pivot counts, not just wall time.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Application in this project
Refactorization policy Trigger on fill-factor or estimated condition; policy is a parameter, not hardcoded
Tolerance ladder Three distinct tolerances (pivot, feasibility, optimality); misuse causes spurious results
Memory layout for performance Column-major f64 buffers; CSC for the basis matrix avoids row scatter
Graceful numerical degradation Status::Numerical returned instead of panicking when LU fails
Property-based testing Random LP instances; dense tableau == revised path on every random instance
Conformance testing Netlib LP set is the production test suite; match reference optima within 1e-6
Differential testing Dense tableau vs. revised simplex vs. reference solver (GLPK) on same inputs

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Project Connection
#277 SAT Solver Two-phase method ↔ unit propagation + DPLL; infeasibility certificates ↔ resolution proofs
#278 SMT Solver LP feasibility as a theory solver; Simplex-T in DPLL(T)
#206 Query Optimizer Cost-model LP relaxations for join ordering; shadow prices as marginal costs
#313 Query Optimizer Same cost-model LP relaxation connection
#372 Classical ML SVM dual formulation is a QP; LP relaxation of SVM is a direct consumer
#369 Graph Algorithms Network-flow LPs; min-cost flow as a degenerate LP instance

2.6 Calibration Value

A working simplex implementation — not a thin wrapper around GLPK — requires the builder to have internalized: matrix factorization, floating-point numerical hygiene, certificate-based correctness, dual theory, and warm-start API design. These skills transfer directly to database query planning (cost-model LP), compiler register allocation (ILP relaxation), and any system requiring certified feasibility proofs.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After building this, you can demonstrably:

  • Walk vertices of a polytope as bases and pivot between adjacent vertices — built in Phase 3 (tableau + pivot) and Phase 4 (primal Phase II).
  • Reason about reduced cost as a price signal to drive entering-variable selection to optimality — built in Phase 4 and Phase 6 (pricing/anti-cycling).
  • Guarantee termination on degenerate problems with Bland's rule and lexicographic perturbation — built in Phase 6 (anti-cycling).
  • Factor the basis instead of inverting it with sparse LU and Forrest-Tomlin rank-1 updates (FTRAN/BTRAN) — built in Phase 9 (revised simplex) and Phase 10 (LU).
  • Produce and verify duality certificates for optimality, infeasibility, and unboundedness — built in Phase 7 (certificates) and Phase 11 (dual simplex).
  • Warm-start reoptimization via the dual simplex for RHS/bound perturbations — built in Phase 11 (dual + warm start).
  • Practice floating-point numerical hygiene with a tolerance hierarchy and condition-aware refactorization — built in Phase 12 (presolve/scaling) and Phase 14 (hardening/conformance).

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                      LINEAR PROGRAMMING / SIMPLEX SOLVER
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|   MPS / LP file ---> lp-parse ---> LpProblem (objective, A (CSC), rhs, bounds)       |
|                                        |                                            |
|                                        v                                            |
|   lp-presolve (remove singletons, tighten bounds, redundancy) + scaling             |
|                                        |                                            |
|                                        v                                            |
|   lp-model: STANDARD FORM (add slack/surplus/artificial; normalize to minimize)      |
|                                        |                                            |
|                                        v                                            |
|   +------------------ lp-simplex (primal driver) ------------------+                 |
|   |  Phase I (two-phase feasibility) -> Phase II (optimize)        |                 |
|   |       |                 |                  |                   |                 |
|   |       v                 v                  v                   |                 |
|   |  lp-pricing        lp-bounds          lp-tableau (dense)       |                 |
|   |  (Dantzig/Bland/   (nonbasic at        OR                      |                 |
|   |   Devex)            lb/ub, flips)     lp-revised (FTRAN/BTRAN) |                 |
|   |                                          |                     |                 |
|   |                                          v                     |                 |
|   |                                     lp-lu (basis LU +          |                 |
|   |                                      Forrest-Tomlin update,    |                 |
|   |                                      refactorize)              |                 |
|   +-----------------------+--------------------------+-------------+                 |
|       warm-start          |                          |                               |
|       v                   v                          v                               |
|   lp-dual (reoptimize)  Solution {x, duals, status}  lp-sensitivity (ranging,         |
|                                |                      shadow prices)                  |
|                                v                                                     |
|   lp-cli / Certificate (KKT optimal | primal ray unbounded | dual ray infeasible)    |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

lp-parse: Accepts MPS (fixed-width + free-format) and LP (CPLEX-style) files from disk or Read trait. Produces LpProblem with sparse objective, CSC constraint matrix, RHS vector, relation vector, and per-variable Bound. No solving logic. Trust boundary: untrusted file input; validates dimensions and bound sanity before emitting. Outputs LpProblem into the presolve pipeline or directly into lp-model for CLI passthrough.

lp-presolve: Receives LpProblem; applies passes: remove empty rows/cols, substitute singleton rows, tighten bounds from implied bounds, drop redundant constraints, fix variables pinned by bounds. Applies geometric or Curtis-Reid scaling to the matrix to improve condition number. Records an ordered postsolve stack of inverse transformations. Outputs a reduced LpProblem + PresolveSolution (for postsolve mapping). Invariant I9 is enforced here: the presolved optimum must equal the original optimum.

lp-model: Converts LpProblem to StandardForm: negates c if Maximize, adds slack columns for Le constraints, surplus+artificial for Ge, artificial-only for Eq, handles free variables by split or bounded treatment, shifts lower bounds to zero. Records var_map: Vec<VarId> from each standard-form column back to the original problem. Outputs StandardForm consumed by lp-simplex. Invariant I10 is enforced here.

lp-simplex: Top-level primal driver. Phase I builds an auxiliary problem minimizing the sum of artificials; delegates to the pivot engine until residual = 0 (feasible) or > 0 (infeasible, emit Certificate::DualRay). Phase II iterates: select entering variable via lp-pricing, compute RatioTestResult via lp-tableau or lp-revised, pivot, check optimality. Detects unboundedness (no leaving variable) and emits Certificate::PrimalRay. Returns Solution.

lp-pricing: Stateless pivot-rule module. Dantzig selects most-negative reduced cost; Bland selects smallest index with negative reduced cost (anti-cycling guarantee); Devex maintains approximate steepest-edge weights for faster convergence. Selects the entering variable column given a reduced-cost slice. Auto-switches to Bland after a stall threshold is detected (P3, I7).

lp-bounds: Bounded-variable ratio-test logic. Given entering column direction, computes RatioTestResult accounting for nonbasic variables at lower or upper bounds and the possibility of a bound flip (entering hits its opposite bound before any basic variable hits a bound). Reduces effective problem size by keeping general bounds native rather than adding rows.

lp-tableau: Dense (m × n) f64 tableau. Reference engine only (P4). Stores current basis columns explicitly. pivot(entering, leaving) normalizes the pivot row then does full Gaussian elimination on all other rows + the objective row. Used for differential testing against lp-revised. Never used for large problems. No persistence.

lp-revised: Production simplex pivot path. Does not store the full tableau. On each iteration: BTRAN (y = c_B B^{-1}) to get dual variables; price reduced costs r_j = c_j - y a_j; FTRAN (d = B^{-1} a_j) for the entering column direction; hand d to lp-bounds for ratio test; update basis. Delegates factorization and solves to lp-lu. Memory O(nnz) not O(m × n).

lp-lu: Sparse LU factorization of the basis matrix B with partial pivoting for numerical stability. Supports FTRAN (forward solve Bx = v) and BTRAN (backward solve B^T y = w) as the core linear-algebra primitives. On each pivot, applies a Forrest-Tomlin (or Bartels-Golub) rank-1 eta-vector update instead of refactoring from scratch. Monitors fill factor and estimated condition; triggers full refactorization when either exceeds threshold. This is the numerical heart of the solver; I1, I2, I3, P2 are all enforced here.

lp-dual: Dual simplex driver. Assumes dual-feasible starting basis (all reduced costs optimal-sign). Selects leaving variable (most-infeasible basic row); computes dual direction; selects entering via bounded dual ratio test to maintain dual feasibility. Ideal for warm-start after RHS or bound perturbation (P8). Accepts a saved Basis object, bypasses Phase I entirely.

lp-sensitivity: Post-optimal module. Given the optimal Basis and Solution, computes: cost-coefficient ranging (allowable increase/decrease in each c_j before the basis changes), RHS ranging (allowable change in each b_i), shadow prices (the dual values y), and reduced-cost ranges. Returns Sensitivity. No re-solving required — all computed from B^{-1} already available in lp-lu.

lp-cli: Binary crate. Subcommands: solve (parse → presolve → model → simplex → postsolve → print solution + certificate), sensitivity (solve + sensitivity analysis), convert (MPS ↔ LP format), check (read a claimed solution, verify KKT conditions, print pass/fail). Reads solver options from CLI flags (tolerance, pivot rule, iteration limit, warm-start basis file). Orchestrates the full pipeline per P7.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes
Problem size — small ≤ 1 000 vars × 1 000 constraints Dense tableau path acceptable
Problem size — medium ≤ 10 000 vars × 10 000 constraints Revised simplex + LU required
Problem size — large (stretch) ≤ 100 000 vars × 50 000 constraints Requires sparse LU + presolve
Netlib conformance All 95 Netlib LP instances, objective within 1e-6 relative Infeasible/unbounded classification exact
Pivot throughput — dense path ≥ 50 000 pivots/s on a 500×500 tableau Single core, x86_64, no SIMD required
Pivot throughput — revised path ≥ 200 000 pivots/s on 1 000-constraint basis Single core; FTRAN/BTRAN dominate
Wall time — small LP (≤200 vars) p50 ≤ 5 ms, p99 ≤ 20 ms Including parse + presolve + solve
Wall time — medium LP (1 000 vars) p50 ≤ 500 ms, p99 ≤ 2 s Revised path; well-conditioned
Warm-start reoptimize ≤ 10% of cold-start time for ±5% RHS perturbation Dual simplex; no Phase I
Memory — dense tableau ≤ 8 × m × n bytes + 20% overhead For m = n = 1 000: ≤ 9.6 MB
Memory — revised simplex ≤ 32 × nnz(B) bytes basis storage nnz(B) ≈ 5–10× m typical
Memory — LU factorization Fill factor ≤ 5× nnz(B) before refactorization trigger Forrest-Tomlin updates bounded
Refactorization frequency Every 100–200 pivots, or when fill factor > 5× or condition estimate > 1e8 Configurable via solver options
Feasibility tolerance 1e-7 (default); configurable down to 1e-10 Used in ratio test and primal-feasibility checks
Optimality tolerance 1e-7 (default); configurable Reduced-cost sign test
Pivot tolerance 1e-4 (Markowitz threshold); configurable Prevents near-singular pivots
Iteration limit Default 100 000; configurable Returns Status::IterationLimit
Startup time ≤ 50 ms cold (binary load + parse + presolve for small LP) CLI subcommand
Shutdown / cleanup Deterministic drop; no background threads; no unsafe memory leaks Verified by Miri in CI
CPU assumptions x86_64 or aarch64; f64 IEEE 754; no SIMD intrinsics required SIMD optional stretch goal
File descriptor budget ≤ 3 FDs per solve (stdin/stdout/stderr or input file + 2) No temp files in core path
Max concurrency Single-threaded solver; no Send required on Basis in Phase 0–13 Sync wrapper for future parallel use
Storage — basis file (warm start) ≤ 24 × m bytes (basic variable indices + status + a few f64 scalars) Binary or JSON; format TBD in Phase 11
KKT verification overhead ≤ 5% of solve time Runs after every optimal; not optional
Sensitivity analysis overhead ≤ 10% of solve time Reuses B^{-1} already in lp-lu
Differential test agreement Dense tableau == revised simplex within 1e-9 on all shared test instances Enforced in CI property-based tests
Certificate verification Primal ray: c · d < -1e-8 and Ad = 0; dual ray: A^T y ≥ 0, b · y < 0 Checked in lp-cli check
Scaling effect Curtis-Reid scaling reduces condition number by ≥ 10× on ill-conditioned Netlib instances Measured, not assumed
Presolve reduction ≥ 20% variable/constraint reduction on typical LP instances with structure Measured on Netlib
Numerical failure budget Status::Numerical returned ≤ 0.1% of Netlib runs Not a panic; graceful degradation

4.2 Tolerance Hierarchy

pivot_tol (1e-4)  <  feasibility_tol (1e-7)  <  optimality_tol (1e-7)
  • pivot_tol: minimum absolute pivot element; reject smaller pivots as numerically unsafe (I1).
  • feasibility_tol: maximum violation of Ax = b or bound constraint at a basic solution (I2, I3).
  • optimality_tol: maximum negative reduced cost accepted as optimal (I4).
  • Comparing any f64 to exact 0.0 against these tolerances is forbidden (P6).

4.3 Failure Budget and Recovery

Failure mode Detection Response
Near-singular pivot ` pivot
LU fill explosion fill_factor > 5× Refactorize immediately
Condition estimate degraded cond_est > 1e8 Refactorize; log warning
Iteration limit reached iters >= limit Return Status::IterationLimit; no panic
Phase I residual > feasibility_tol At Phase I termination Return Status::Infeasible + dual ray
Unexpected NaN/inf in basis Any FTRAN/BTRAN result Return Status::Numerical; log
Parse error in MPS/LP file During lp-parse Return Err(ParseError); no partial state

5. Required Reading

Books

B1 — Dantzig — Linear Programming and Extensions (Princeton, 1963)

  • Chapters: 1–5 (simplex derivation, tableau mechanics, standard form); 7 (duality); 17 (degenerate pivots); 20 (bounded-variable extensions)
  • Concepts: Original pivot rule derivation; standard-form convention (slack + surplus + artificial); primal/dual complementarity; bounded-variable extension; the geometric interpretation of a basis as a vertex of the feasible polytope
  • Why read: Every subsequent textbook and implementation descends from this; reading it directly prevents cargo-culting intermediate sources; the bounded-variable chapter is the direct ancestor of lp-bounds

B2 — Chvátal — Linear Programming (W.H. Freeman, 1983)

  • Chapters: 3 (simplex algorithm); 4–5 (duality, complementary slackness); 7 (sensitivity analysis); 9 (Bland's rule proof + degeneracy); 10 (two-phase method); 11 (Big-M)
  • Concepts: Exact finite-termination proof of Bland's rule (required for I7); sensitivity ranging derivation for cost coefficients and RHS intervals (lp-sensitivity); complementary slackness conditions used to construct Certificate::KktOptimal (P1, P5); two-phase feasibility vs. Big-M tradeoffs
  • Why read: Canonical teaching text; proofs are rigorous and directly implementable; Chapter 9 is the definitive anti-cycling reference; sensitivity chapter maps directly onto Sensitivity return type

B3 — Bertsimas & Tsitsiklis — Introduction to Linear Optimization (Athena Scientific, 1997)

  • Chapters: 3 (simplex); 4 (duality, Farkas lemma); 5 (revised simplex, FTRAN/BTRAN); 6 (sensitivity); 7 (degeneracy, anti-cycling, perturbation method, complexity); 9 (network simplex — context only)
  • Concepts: Revised simplex derivation from first principles; formal FTRAN/BTRAN framework as triangular solves against an LU basis; lexicographic anti-cycling; perturbation method; Farkas' lemma proof for Certificate::DualRay (I8, P5); complexity bound argument
  • Why read: Best formal treatment of revised simplex and FTRAN/BTRAN; Farkas' lemma chapter underpins the infeasibility certificate; anti-cycling chapter gives the lexicographic method as an alternative to Bland

B4 — Maros — Computational Techniques of the Simplex Method (Kluwer, 2003)

  • Chapters: 2 (data structures for basis/tableau, CSC storage); 3 (pricing: Dantzig, Devex, steepest-edge); 4 (ratio test, stability); 5 (bounded-variable simplex); 6 (LU factorization, Forrest-Tomlin, Bartels-Golub updates, refactorization); 7 (numerical stability, pivot thresholds, Markowitz criterion); 8 (presolve + Curtis-Reid scaling); 9 (dual simplex)
  • Concepts: THE implementation reference; all of lp-lu, lp-bounds, lp-pricing, lp-presolve draw directly from here; refactorization trigger criteria (condition estimate, fill factor); Devex weight update formula; bounded-variable ratio test with bound flips; Markowitz pivot selection for fill reduction; Curtis-Reid iterative scaling
  • Why read: No other single source covers all the implementation decisions in this project; Chapters 5–7 are required reading before writing a single line of lp-lu or lp-bounds

B5 — Golub & Van Loan — Matrix Computations, 4th ed. (Johns Hopkins, 2013)

  • Chapters: 3 (LU factorization, partial pivoting, growth factor bounds); 4 (rank-1 updates, Sherman-Morrison); 5.6 (sparse triangular solves); 12 (sparse systems)
  • Concepts: Stability guarantees for LU with partial pivoting; growth-factor bound for PIVOT_TOL selection; triangular solve algorithms for FTRAN/BTRAN; numerical conditioning theory underlying P2
  • Why read: Provides the theoretical grounding that justifies lp-lu's design; growth-factor analysis explains why PIVOT_TOL ≥ 0.01 is necessary

B6 — Nocedal & Wright — Numerical Optimization, 2nd ed. (Springer, 2006)

  • Chapters: 13 (LP theory, vertex enumeration); 14 (simplex internals, degeneracy); 16 (active-set methods — context for bounded variables)
  • Concepts: KKT conditions stated precisely for LP; optimality tolerance justification; reduced-gradient interpretation of Dantzig pricing; connection between KKT and Certificate::KktOptimal
  • Why read: Bridges LP and nonlinear optimization; Chapter 14's implementation discussion complements Maros (B4) with a modern algorithmic perspective

B7 — Schrijver — Theory of Linear and Integer Programming (Wiley, 1986)

  • Chapters: 7 (Farkas' lemma and alternative systems); 8 (duality theorems, strong duality); 9 (LP algorithms and complexity)
  • Concepts: Rigorous Farkas' lemma derivation for Certificate::DualRay; strong duality theorem underlying I5; Fourier-Motzkin elimination as a presolve concept
  • Why read: Most complete theoretical treatment of infeasibility certificates; I8 verification logic requires this level of rigor

B8 — Vanderbei — Linear Programming: Foundations and Extensions, 4th ed. (Springer, 2014)

  • Chapters: 5 (sensitivity analysis — cost-ranging and RHS-ranging derivations); 13 (revised simplex); 18 (degeneracy and anti-cycling); 21 (bounded variables)
  • Concepts: Shadow-price derivation aligned directly with Sensitivity.shadow_prices; cost-coefficient ranging formula; RHS ranging; degenerate-case sensitivity caveats (ranges may degenerate to a point — must be handled in lp-sensitivity)
  • Why read: Most accessible sensitivity-analysis derivation; Chapter 18 covers perturbation method as a practical alternative to Bland

B9 — Wright — Primal-Dual Interior-Point Methods (SIAM, 1997)

  • Chapters: 1–2 (LP geometry, polytope); 5 (complementarity, duality gap); Appendix A (presolve reductions transferable to simplex)
  • Concepts: Interior-point stretch-goal framing; complementary slackness overlap with KKT; shared presolve reductions (singleton rows, bound tightening) applicable to lp-presolve
  • Why read: Provides context for the stretch-goal interior-point path; presolve appendix directly informs lp-presolve's reduction list

Papers

P1 — Dantzig (1951), Cowles Commission Monograph 13

  • Title: "Maximization of a linear function of variables subject to linear inequalities"
  • Contribution: First formal simplex algorithm
  • Relevance: Origin of lp-tableau pivot loop; standard-form convention; optimality termination criterion

P2 — Bland (1977), Mathematics of Operations Research 2(2)

  • Title: "New finite pivoting rules for the simplex method"
  • Contribution: Smallest-index entering and leaving variable guarantees finite termination
  • Relevance: Direct algorithm for PivotRule::Bland in lp-pricing; proof of I7; stall-detection switch logic in lp-simplex

P3 — Bartels & Golub (1969), CACM 12(5)

  • Title: "The Simplex Method of Linear Programming Using LU Decomposition"
  • Contribution: LU basis representation; product-form rank-1 eta updates for pivots; FTRAN/BTRAN as triangular solves
  • Relevance: Core architecture of lp-lu; basis-update structure; the concept of eta vectors for cheap incremental updates

P4 — Forrest & Tomlin (1972), Mathematical Programming 2

  • Title: "Updated Triangular Factors of the Basis to Maintain Sparsity in the Product Form Simplex Method"
  • Contribution: Forrest-Tomlin update: sparsity-preserving rank-1 update to the U factor
  • Relevance: lp-lu update path; fill-control strategy during a pivot sequence; refactorization trigger when fill degrades; the key advance over Bartels-Golub for sparse matrices

P5 — Harris (1973), Mathematical Programming 5

  • Title: "Pivot selection methods of the Devex LP code"
  • Contribution: Devex approximate steepest-edge pricing weights with cheap update formula
  • Relevance: PivotRule::Devex in lp-pricing; weight update formula per pivot; convergence behavior vs. Dantzig; computational overhead vs. exact steepest-edge

P6 — Goldfarb & Forrest (1992), Mathematical Programming 57

  • Title: "Steepest-edge simplex algorithms for linear programming"
  • Contribution: Exact steepest-edge weight computation and maintenance
  • Relevance: Theoretical basis for lp-pricing steepest-edge mode; comparison with Devex approximation; iteration-count reduction on degenerate problems

P7 — Reid (1982), Mathematical Programming 24

  • Title: "A sparsity-exploiting variant of the Bartels-Golub decomposition for LP bases"
  • Contribution: Sparse LU with Markowitz column reordering for fill reduction
  • Relevance: lp-lu column ordering before factorization; Markowitz threshold criterion (accept pivot if |aij| ≥ PIVOT_TOL * row_max); memory allocation strategy for L/U factors

P8 — Suhl & Suhl (1993), ORSA Journal on Computing 5(3)

  • Title: "Computing sparse LU factorizations for large-scale LP bases"
  • Contribution: Practical pivot heuristics for sparse LP basis LU; update fill management
  • Relevance: lp-lu Markowitz pivot selection; memory layout for eta vectors; update count threshold for triggering refactorization

P9 — Curtis & Reid (1972), Journal of the Institute of Mathematics and Its Applications 10

  • Title: "On the automatic scaling of matrices for Gaussian elimination"
  • Contribution: Curtis-Reid iterative scaling algorithm; equilibrates row/column norms in the log-mean sense
  • Relevance: lp-presolve scaling implementation; convergence criterion for iterative scaling loop; reduces condition number before factorization

P10 — Andersen & Andersen (1995), Mathematical Programming 71

  • Title: "Presolving in Linear Programming"
  • Contribution: Systematic presolve reductions: empty rows/cols, singleton rows, bound tightening, forcing constraints, redundancy detection
  • Relevance: lp-presolve reduction catalogue; PostsolveStack operation types and their invertibility conditions; I9 fidelity argument

P11 — Tomlin (1974), Mathematical Programming 6

  • Title: "On pricing and backward transformation in linear programming"
  • Contribution: BTRAN pricing for the dual simplex; reduced-cost computation as rc = c - y A after BTRAN
  • Relevance: lp-dual pricing path; dual ratio test setup; the computational advantage of BTRAN over re-computing from scratch

P12 — Koberstein (2005), PhD thesis, Universität Paderborn

  • Title: "The dual simplex method, techniques for a fast and stable implementation"
  • Contribution: Dual simplex ratio test stability; bound-flipping ratio test for dual; refactorization schedule; warm-start pivot sequence
  • Relevance: lp-dual implementation details; bounded-dual ratio test; warm-start pivot sequence (P8); degeneracy handling in dual context

P13 — Gill, Murray, Saunders, Wright (1987), Linear Algebra and Its Applications 88/89

  • Title: "Maintaining LU factors of a general sparse matrix"
  • Contribution: Stable rank-1 updates to LU factors under numerical perturbation; growth-factor monitoring
  • Relevance: lp-lu update stability; when to abandon eta updates and refactorize; growth-factor criteria for P2

P14 — Tomlin (1972), Mathematical Programming 2

  • Title: "Updated triangular factors of the basis" (companion to Forrest-Tomlin)
  • Contribution: Systematic comparison of product-form vs. updated-factor methods; refactorization frequency analysis
  • Relevance: Justifies the choice of Forrest-Tomlin over pure product-form for lp-lu; refactorization interval selection

P15 — Murtagh (1981) and companion papers

  • Title: Advanced Linear Programming: Computation and Practice + associated papers
  • Contribution: Bounded-variable ratio test with finite lower/upper bounds; nonbasic-at-bound status tracking
  • Relevance: lp-bounds ratio test with bound-flip condition (I6); VarStatus::NonbasicLower/Upper logic; reduces basis size vs. adding explicit bound rows

Reference Implementations

R1 — GLPK (GNU Linear Programming Kit, glpk-5.x)

  • Files: src/simplex/glpspx01.c (primal simplex driver), src/simplex/glpspx02.c (dual simplex driver), src/bflib/ (LU + Forrest-Tomlin: glpbfd.c, glplux.c), src/mps/glpmps.c (MPS reader), src/lp/glplpx.c (problem builder)
  • Lessons: Clean primal/dual driver separation; Forrest-Tomlin update mechanics in glpbfd.c; MPS reader handles all BOUNDS types including free (FR/MI) and fixed (FX); the anti-cycling stall-detect switch is explicit
  • Pitfalls: C macros obscure control flow; dense/revised decision is a compile-time global flag, not runtime dispatch; monolithic file structure with deep coupling; do not copy the style, copy only the algorithm logic

R2 — HiGHS (ERGO-Code/HiGHS)

  • Files: src/simplex/HSimplexDual.* (dual simplex), src/simplex/HEkkPrimal.* (primal + Devex), src/lp_data/HighsLp.* (LP data model), src/presolve/Presolve.* (presolve stack), src/util/HFactorization.* (LU basis), src/simplex/HSimplex.cpp (dispatch)
  • Lessons: Modern dual-simplex-first philosophy; modular pricing/ratio-test interfaces; presolve as a PresolveStack with typed operations analogous to PostsolveStack; Devex pricing implementation in HEkkPrimal; named tolerances throughout
  • Pitfalls: Depends on Abseil / proto infrastructure; some global mutable state in HFactor; template use not directly portable to Rust trait design; some algorithms assume dense-column-indexed variable numbering

R3 — CLP (COIN-OR Linear Programming)

  • Files: src/ClpSimplex.cpp (main driver), src/ClpFactorization.cpp (LU basis), src/ClpPresolve.cpp (presolve chain), src/ClpPrimalColumnSteepest.cpp (steepest-edge), src/ClpDualRowSteepest.cpp (dual steepest-edge)
  • Lessons: Bounded-variable handling as first-class; steepest-edge weight update loop shows the per-pivot maintenance cost; presolve as a linked list of CoinPresolveAction objects — each action records its inverse for postsolve
  • Pitfalls: Very dense C++ virtual dispatch; factor code mixes OSL (IBM) legacy; CoinPresolveAction object graph is hard to navigate without the full COIN-OR build infrastructure

R4 — lp_solve 5.5

  • Files: lp_simplex.c (primal/dual loop), lp_price.c (Dantzig/Devex pricing toggle), lp_rlp.c (MPS/LP reader), lp_scale.c (scaling pre-pass), lp_crash.c (basis crash heuristic)
  • Lessons: Compact pricing module shows Dantzig/Devex toggle cleanly; scaling as a stand-alone pre-pass; LP reader covers free rows and section-ordering edge cases; basis crash for warm starting
  • Pitfalls: Tolerances hardcoded as magic numbers (violates P6); limited anti-cycling protection; numeric issues on ill-conditioned Netlib instances; monolithic file structure

R5 — Netlib LP test set (netlib.org/lp/data/)

  • Files: All *.mps files in the main set; index and readme; infeas/ subdirectory for infeasible instances
  • Lessons: Ground-truth optimal objectives for Phase 14 conformance; infeasible/unbounded cases test certificate extraction; diverse problem structures (network flow, blending, transportation, scheduling)
  • Pitfalls: Objectives vary by ~1e-7 across reference solvers — use tolerance-based comparison, not exact float equality; some files use fixed-format MPS with strict column positions that break free-format parsers

R6 — SOPLEX (ZIB, soplex.zib.de)

  • Files: src/spxsolver.cpp (main driver), src/spxpricer.cpp (pricing interface), src/spxratiotester.cpp (ratio test interface), src/slufactor.cpp (LU), src/spxbasis.cpp (basis management)
  • Lessons: Abstract SPxPricer / SPxRatioTester / SPxBasis interfaces inspire lp-pricing / lp-bounds Rust trait design; ratio tester encapsulation; basis status tracking via explicit enum; pricing strategy is a swappable object
  • Pitfalls: Virtual dispatch overhead; naming conventions differ; skip the SoPlex-specific entering/leaving pair protocol — it is tailored to their internal representation

R7 — Google OR-Tools GLOP (github.com/google/or-tools, ortools/glop/)

  • Files: revised_simplex.cc (main solver), lu_factorization.cc (LU with Forrest-Tomlin), preprocessor.cc (presolve), entering_variable.cc (pricing), reduced_costs.cc (BTRAN + reduced-cost management)
  • Lessons: Well-typed C++ with named tolerances (closely mirrors P6); preprocessor stage structure with a PresolveStack; EnteringVariable as a strategy class; ReducedCosts as a separate concern from the solver
  • Pitfalls: Depends on Abseil and protobuf infrastructure; some algorithms tied to dense-index assumptions; proto-based solution type does not map directly to this project's Solution type

R8 — Forrest's "Notes on the Clp dual simplex" (PDF, available from COIN-OR)

  • Content: Practical dual simplex warm-start design; bound-flipping ratio test detail; refactorization frequency heuristic; numerical failure modes and recovery
  • Lessons: Read alongside R3 code; explains design decisions not obvious from source alone; bound-flipping in the dual context; when to declare numerical failure vs. retry
  • Pitfalls: Not a code file; design rationale only; some details are specific to CLP's internal data layout

R9 — Achterberg — SCIP Thesis (2007), TU Berlin

  • Chapters: LP relaxation section (Ch. 2), warm-start and reoptimization discussion
  • Lessons: Context for how this LP solver feeds into a MIP tree search; dual simplex warm-start frequency and cost; LP relaxation quality requirements for branching
  • Pitfalls: SCIP is a MIP solver; LP-specific sections are a small fraction; read only the LP relaxation and warm-start chapters

RFCs / Specifications

S1 — MPS Format Specification (IBM MPS + Extended MPS)

  • Sections: ROWS (free-row N, LE, GE, EQ types); COLUMNS (variable entries, integer markers); RHS; BOUNDS (LO, UP, FX, FR, MI, BV, LI, UI types); RANGES (modifies constraint bounds to create intervals); OBJSENSE (overrides minimize default)
  • Usage: lp-parse must handle all bound types; RANGES row creates [rhs, rhs + |range|] or [rhs - |range|, rhs] intervals depending on sign and constraint type; reference glpmps.c (R1) for edge cases including blank-field handling and integer markers

S2 — LP Format (CPLEX/lp_solve dialect)

  • Sections: Objective section (minimize/maximize keyword); Constraints section (>=, <=, = with named constraints); Bounds section (default 0 ≤ x < +∞; free keyword for unbounded; explicit l ≤ x ≤ u); General / Binary / SOS sections; \ comment syntax
  • Usage: lp-parse LP reader; free-row naming (obj: prefix); multi-line constraint continuation; variable name parsing (alphanumeric + _); whitespace handling in >= vs. > =

S3 — IEEE 754-2019 Floating-Point Arithmetic

  • Sections: Sec. 4 (infinity and NaN representation and propagation); Sec. 5.7 (comparison predicates — distinguishes <, ≤, =, unordered); Sec. 7 (exceptions: overflow, underflow, invalid, division by zero, inexact)
  • Usage: f64::INFINITY used for free-variable bounds; f64::NEG_INFINITY for lower bound of free vars; NaN in matrix operations is a correctness failure — all FTRAN/BTRAN results must be checked for NaN after debug builds; infinity propagation in ratio test must be guarded (bound of +inf → ratio = +inf → never the minimum ratio); all comparisons use named tolerances not literal 0.0 (P6)

S4 — KKT Optimality Conditions for LP

  • Reference: Chvátal Ch. 5 (B2); Bertsimas Ch. 4 (B3); Nocedal & Wright Ch. 13 (B6)
  • Statement: Primal feasibility (Ax = b, l ≤ x ≤ u); dual feasibility (rc = c - y A with rcⱼ ≥ 0 for NonbasicLower, rcⱼ ≤ 0 for NonbasicUpper, rcⱼ = 0 for basic); complementary slackness ((xⱼ - lⱼ)·rcⱼ = 0, (uⱼ - xⱼ)·rcⱼ = 0)
  • Usage: Certificate::KktOptimal construction; P1 post-solve verification; I4 and I5 simultaneous check; all three conditions checked within their respective tolerances (FEAS_TOL, OPT_TOL)

S5 — Farkas' Lemma

  • Reference: Schrijver Ch. 7 (B7); Bertsimas Ch. 4 (B3)
  • Statement (infeasibility form): {Ax = b, x ≥ 0} infeasible iff ∃y: yᵀA ≥ 0, yᵀb < 0
  • Usage: Certificate::DualRay for Status::Infeasible; I8 certificate validity check; lp-dual and lp-simplex Phase I infeasibility detection and ray extraction; dual-ray direction extracted from the Phase I auxiliary problem's optimal dual solution

6. Design Principles

P1 — Certified Optimality

A reported Status::Optimal is verified by KKT conditions and carries Certificate::KktOptimal; the solver proves its answer rather than asserting it.

  • Explanation. Three simultaneous checks before returning Optimal:
    1. Primal feasibility: ‖Ax - b‖∞ ≤ FEAS_TOL and lⱼ - FEAS_TOL ≤ xⱼ ≤ uⱼ + FEAS_TOL for all j
    2. Dual feasibility: rcⱼ ≥ -OPT_TOL for NonbasicLower, rcⱼ ≤ OPT_TOL for NonbasicUpper, |rcⱼ| ≤ OPT_TOL for basic
    3. Complementary slackness: |(xⱼ - lⱼ) · rcⱼ| ≤ OPT_TOL · max(1, |xⱼ|) for all j
  • Tradeoff. Post-solve KKT check is O(m + n); negligible vs. total pivot cost. No performance justification for omission.
  • Operational. lp-simplex calls verify_kkt(solution, problem, tolerances) before returning. Failure upgrades status to Status::Numerical — never a silent wrong answer.
  • Violation consequence. Reporting an incorrect optimum; downstream LP-relaxation bounds in a MIP solver are unsound; lp-sensitivity shadow prices are wrong; Phase 14 conformance failures on Netlib instances where the true optimum is known.
  • Example of violation. Dual simplex converges (all reduced costs correctly signed) but primal feasibility was never verified after warm-start; solver returns Status::Optimal with a primal-infeasible solution.

P2 — Numerical Stability over Naive Speed

The basis is held as an LU factorization with partial pivoting in lp-lu; refactorization is triggered proactively before condition degrades.

  • Explanation. Stability enforced by Markowitz threshold: accept pivot element |aᵢⱼ| only if |aᵢⱼ| ≥ PIVOT_TOL · max_{k}|aₖⱼ| where PIVOT_TOL ≥ 0.01. Refactorize from scratch when: n_updates ≥ MAX_ETA_UPDATES (suggested: 100–200), or estimated condition exceeds REFACT_COND_THRESHOLD (suggested: 1e12), or a post-pivot residual ‖B·B⁻¹eₖ - eₖ‖ > REFACT_RESID_TOL.
  • Tradeoff. Refactorization costs O(nnz(B)·m) every ~100–200 pivots; prevents catastrophic cancellation in FTRAN/BTRAN that would produce wrong reduced costs and wrong ratios.
  • Operational. lp-lu::should_refactorize() -> bool checked after every pivot in lp-revised. lp-lu::refactorize(B_cols) rebuilds from current basis columns.
  • Violation consequence. Basis drift: reduced costs lose sign reliability; solver reports a wrong optimum (I4 fails) or enters a degenerate loop on near-singular basis (I1 fails); KKT verification catches this as Status::Numerical if P1 is also enforced.
  • Example of violation. Accumulating 500 eta-vector updates without refactorization on a 200×200 LP; floating-point growth factor exceeds 1e8; FTRAN produces near-zero step sizes; solver reports optimal at a non-optimal vertex.

P3 — Guaranteed Termination

Anti-cycling is always enabled; an infinite loop is a correctness failure, not an acceptable outcome.

  • Explanation. Default pricing: Dantzig (most negative reduced cost). After STALL_DETECT_WINDOW (suggested: 20) consecutive degenerate pivots (step size ≤ FEAS_TOL), switch to PivotRule::Bland (smallest VarId entering, smallest VarId leaving). The lexicographic/perturbation method is an available alternative when Bland produces too many pivots.
  • Stall detection table:
Condition Action
degenerate_count < STALL_DETECT_WINDOW Continue with current rule
degenerate_count ≥ STALL_DETECT_WINDOW Switch to PivotRule::Bland
iteration_count ≥ MAX_ITER Return Status::IterationLimit
Progress after Bland switch Reset degenerate_count, switch back to Dantzig
  • Tradeoff. Bland may require more pivots on non-degenerate problems; only active after stall detection, minimizing overhead in typical use. Perturbation is faster in practice but more complex.
  • Operational. lp-pricing::select_entering(rule, rc, var_ids) -> Option<usize> dispatches on rule; lp-simplex maintains degenerate_count: usize.
  • Violation consequence. Infinite loop on Beale's cycling LP, Kuhn's cycling example, or any degenerate network-flow LP; Phase 14 cycling stress test hangs indefinitely.
  • Example of violation. Dantzig with no stall detection applied to Beale's 6-variable, 4-constraint cycling LP; solver cycles through the same 6 bases indefinitely; never terminates.

P4 — Revised over Dense at Scale

The production pivot path uses FTRAN/BTRAN via lp-lu and never materializes the full m×n tableau; lp-tableau is a reference oracle only.

  • Complexity comparison:
Path Memory per iteration Pivot cost When used
lp-tableau (dense) O(m·n) O(m·n) n ≤ DENSE_THRESHOLD or --debug-dense
lp-revised (FTRAN/BTRAN) O(nnz(A) + m) O(nnz(B) + nnz(column)) n > DENSE_THRESHOLD (production)
  • Explanation. lp-revised computes B⁻¹aⱼ (FTRAN: forward triangular solve) and y = cB·B⁻¹ (BTRAN: backward triangular solve) on demand against LuFactorization. Reduced costs for unpriced variables are computed from y as rcⱼ = cⱼ - y·Aⱼ without forming any row of the full tableau.
  • Tradeoff. lp-revised requires correct LU maintenance (P2); more implementation complexity in lp-lu vs. the dense path. lp-tableau shares the same PivotRule and tolerance inputs enabling exact differential testing.
  • Operational. lp-cli selects lp-revised for n > DENSE_THRESHOLD (suggested: 200 variables). Both paths implement the same SimplexEngine trait; switching is a one-line change.
  • Violation consequence. A 1000-variable LP with m = 500 rows requires 500 MB for the dense tableau vs. ~4 MB with the revised path; out-of-memory on standard hardware.
  • Example of violation. Selecting lp-tableau for Netlib instance SHARE2B (96 rows, 162 columns, 723 nonzeros); memory is fine but every pivot costs O(96·162) = 15552 operations vs. O(723/162·96) ≈ 428 operations per FTRAN.

P5 — Every Terminal Status Carries a Certificate

Solution.certificate is non-trivial for Optimal, Infeasible, and Unbounded; always externally verifiable.

  • Certificate type table:
Status Certificate Construction Verification condition
Optimal KktOptimal dual values y, reduced costs rc KKT three conditions (P1)
Unbounded PrimalRay(d) direction from FTRAN on entering column c·d < -OPT_TOL, Ad = 0, d ≥ 0
Infeasible DualRay(y) Phase I dual solution or dual simplex dual ray yᵀb < -OPT_TOL, Aᵀy ≥ -OPT_TOL
IterationLimit None — Partial solution only
Numerical None — Partial solution only
  • Explanation. lp-simplex emits a primal ray when no leaving variable exists on an improving column (unbounded direction confirmed). lp-simplex Phase I emits a dual ray when the auxiliary optimum > FEAS_TOL. lp-dual emits a dual ray when dual infeasible.
  • Tradeoff. Ray construction costs one extra FTRAN (primal ray) or one dual step (dual ray); negligible vs. total solve time.
  • Operational. lp-cli check subcommand calls verify_certificate(cert, problem, tolerances) on the loaded solution; exit code 0 if valid, 1 if invalid.
  • Violation consequence. Caller cannot verify result; MIP solvers relying on LP-relaxation infeasibility proof receive no witness; incorrect branching decisions are silent.
  • Example of violation. Returning Status::Infeasible from Phase I without extracting the dual ray; the caller cannot distinguish a solver bug from genuine infeasibility; no way to extract a minimal infeasible subset.

P6 — Tolerances Are Explicit and Consistent

All floating-point comparisons use named constants from lp-core::Tolerances; literal 0.0 comparisons in numerical code are forbidden.

  • Tolerance table:
Name Default Used in Meaning
FEAS_TOL 1e-7 ratio test, primal feas check, KKT check max allowed primal constraint violation
OPT_TOL 1e-9 optimality check, dual feas, KKT check max allowed wrong-sign reduced cost
PIVOT_TOL 1e-4 LU factorization, Markowitz threshold min acceptable pivot element fraction
REFACT_RESID_TOL 1e-8 basis residual check post-pivot trigger for emergency refactorization
  • Explanation. Tolerances is a required parameter of every solve entry point. A clippy lint (or code-review checklist) bans x == 0.0, x < 0.0, x > 0.0 on f64 in simplex-critical paths.
  • Tradeoff. Tighter OPT_TOL increases the rate of wrong-sign reduced costs detected as violations; tighter FEAS_TOL may reject near-feasible solutions that are acceptable in practice. Defaults match GLPK/HiGHS practice.
  • Violation consequence. Inconsistent tolerances produce disagreement between ratio test (PIVOT_TOL) and KKT check (OPT_TOL); solver may accept a pivot that stability check would reject, or reject a valid optimum.
  • Example of violation. Ratio test uses inline step < 1e-10 (hardcoded) while KKT check uses OPT_TOL = 1e-9; near-degenerate pivots accepted by ratio test but flagged by KKT, producing spurious Status::Numerical on well-conditioned problems.

P7 — Presolve then Solve then Postsolve

The pipeline is strictly reduce → solve → recover; the optimizer never sees the original problem's variable numbering.

  • Pipeline stages:
LpProblem (original)
   → lp-presolve::reduce()   → (LpProblem reduced, PostsolveStack)
   → lp-model::to_standard_form()  → StandardForm
   → lp-simplex / lp-revised  → Solution (reduced space)
   → lp-presolve::postsolve()  → Solution (original space)
  • Explanation. Each presolve reduction records a PostsolveOp variant (FixedVar, SingletonRow, RedundantRow, BoundTighten, ScaleRow, ScaleCol, ...) onto a Vec<PostsolveOp>. Postsolve consumes the stack in LIFO order, applying each inverse operation.
  • Tradeoff. Presolve adds latency for small (< 50 constraint) problems; significant gain for large or structured LPs (network, transportation).
  • Operational. lp-cli orchestrates the pipeline. PostsolveStack is serializable so partial solutions can be recovered after Status::IterationLimit.
  • Violation consequence. Postsolve produces a primal-infeasible original-space solution; objective mismatch; I9 violated; lp-cli check flags this.
  • Example of violation. Singleton-row substitution records the coefficient incorrectly in PostsolveOp::SingletonRow; postsolve recovers the wrong value for a substituted variable; the original-constraint primal feasibility check fails by a factor of 2.

P8 — Warm-Start Friendliness

Basis is serializable and first-class; lp-dual::solve_warm reoptimizes from a saved basis in O(delta pivots).

  • Explanation. Solution includes basis: Basis containing basic: Vec<VarId>, status: Vec<VarStatus>, factor: LuFactorization. The caller serializes this between solves. lp-dual::solve_warm(problem, basis, tolerances) validates dimensions and non-singularity, then runs dual simplex from the warm basis.
  • Warm-start validity checks:
    • basis.basic.len() == m (dimension match)
    • basis.factor.is_valid() (non-singular LU, consistent with current basis columns)
    • All VarStatus entries consistent with the new problem's bound structure
  • Tradeoff. Basis serialization requires serde support for LuFactorization (potentially large); factor re-validation at load adds O(m) overhead.
  • Operational. lp-dual emits a warning and falls back to cold-start primal if warm-start validation fails.
  • Violation consequence. MIP LP-relaxation reoptimization costs O(full re-solve) per tree node instead of O(delta pivots); a 1000-node MIP tree becomes impractical.
  • Example of violation. Discarding Basis after each LP solve in a cutting-plane loop; each iteration re-runs Phase I + Phase II from scratch; runtime increases by 10–100× vs. warm-started dual simplex.

P9 — Bounded Variables Are Native

General lower/upper bounds are handled in the ratio test via VarStatus; no explicit constraint rows are added for variable bounds.

  • Explanation. Each variable sits in one of four states:
VarStatus Meaning Reduced cost sign
Basic In basis; value determined by B⁻¹b Zero (within OPT_TOL)
NonbasicLower Fixed at lower bound rc ≥ 0
NonbasicUpper Fixed at upper bound rc ≤ 0
Free Nonbasic free variable rc = 0 (special)
  • lp-bounds::ratio_test_bounded() considers: (a) a basic variable reaches a bound → RatioTestResult { leaving: Some(j), bound_flip: false }; (b) the entering variable reaches its opposite bound first → RatioTestResult { leaving: None, bound_flip: true }. A bound flip changes VarStatus without a pivot.
  • Tradeoff. Bound-flip logic increases ratio-test complexity; eliminates O(n) artificial rows that would inflate m and increase LU cost by O(m³).
  • Operational. lp-model::to_standard_form() does NOT add bound rows; it preserves bounds in StandardForm.bounds.
  • Violation consequence. Adding explicit bound rows for a 500-variable LP inflates the basis from 200×200 to 700×700; LU factorization cost triples; model is harder to read.
  • Example of violation. Converting 0 ≤ xⱼ ≤ 10 to xⱼ ≤ 10 and -xⱼ ≤ 0 as explicit constraints; basis grows; presolve eventually removes them but adds PostsolveStack entries for no algorithmic benefit.

P10 — Determinism and Reproducibility

Identical inputs yield identical pivot sequences and bit-identical outputs; no randomness or hash-order dependence.

  • Explanation. No random tie-breaking anywhere in the solver. Ties in pricing (equal reduced costs among candidates) are broken by ascending VarId order — the minimum-index rule. SparseMatrix CSC format stores columns in ascending VarId order. All iteration over variable indices uses sorted order. No HashMap<VarId, _> where iteration order matters.
  • Determinism requirements by crate:
Crate Requirement
lp-pricing Tie-break by ascending VarId; no random selection
lp-lu Markowitz pivot selection: deterministic tie-break by row index
lp-presolve Reductions applied in fixed order: empty rows, singletons, bound tightening, redundancy
lp-bounds Tie in ratio test: smallest leaving VarId
lp-core VarId ordering: assigned in problem-builder insertion order; stable
  • Tradeoff. Index-order tie-breaking may not be numerically optimal; acceptable because P3 (anti-cycling) handles the degeneracy that tie-breaking affects.
  • Operational. CI runs full Netlib conformance suite twice sequentially and asserts identical objectives and iteration counts.
  • Violation consequence. Non-reproducible test failures; impossible to bisect numerical regressions; conformance tests produce different Netlib objectives across runs.
  • Example of violation. Using HashMap<VarId, f64> for reduced costs and iterating for pricing; Rust's HashMap uses random seed by default; different entering-variable selections across runs; Bland's rule trigger changes; different pivot counts.

P11 — Numerical Tolerance Discipline

Every floating-point comparison in simplex-critical code names its tolerance from lp-core::Tolerances (FEAS_TOL, OPT_TOL, PIVOT_TOL); a literal == 0.0, < 0.0, or hard-coded epsilon is a defect, not a style nit.

  • Explanation. Three named tolerances gate every simplex decision: FEAS_TOL gates the ratio test and primal-feasibility checks; OPT_TOL gates reduced-cost sign checks and the KKT verifier; PIVOT_TOL gates the Markowitz pivot-acceptance threshold in lp-lu and the ratio-test denominator guard. A comparison written as x == 0.0 or col[i] < 1e-10 (inline literal) bypasses the single point of tolerance configuration and cannot be tuned, logged, or swept (Phase 14's tolerance-sweep test).
  • Tradeoff. Threading a Tolerances (or SolverOpts) parameter through every numerically sensitive function adds a parameter to call sites; the alternative — module-level constants or inline literals — is more convenient locally but produces silent divergence between, e.g., the ratio test's threshold and the KKT verifier's threshold (see P6's example).
  • Operational. A lint pass (clippy float_cmp plus a repo-local grep-based CI check for == 0.0, < 0.0, > 0.0 on f64 outside lp-core::tolerances) runs in the same CI stage as cargo clippy -D warnings (Phase 14). Any new comparison must cite a named constant or fail review.
  • Violation consequence. A hard-coded compare in one crate (e.g., lp-dual's ratio test using 1e-9 while lp-lu uses PIVOT_TOL = 1e-4) produces exactly the disagreement P6 warns about — a pivot accepted by one gate and flagged Status::Numerical by another — and, worse, silently accepts a near-zero denominator that a consistent PIVOT_TOL would have rejected, which is a direct path to false cycling (I7) or a wrong feasibility verdict (I2).
  • Example of violation. lp-revised::price_nonbasic ships with if rc.abs() < 0.0000001 { continue } instead of if rc.abs() < opts.opt_tol; a later global change to OPT_TOL (e.g., loosened for an ill-conditioned Netlib instance) never reaches this comparison, and the solver reports Status::Numerical on a problem that is actually optimal under the new tolerance.

P12 — Basis Factorization Freshness

The LuFactorization backing the current basis is refactorized whenever fill-in, eta-chain growth, or post-pivot residual crosses a named threshold — freshness is checked after every pivot in every phase that consumes a basis (lp-revised, lp-dual, warm-start), not only on the primal hot path.

  • Explanation. Three independent triggers, all evaluated by lp-lu::should_refactorize(): (1) n_updates >= REFACT_UPDATE_LIMIT, (2) estimated condition number > REFACT_COND_THRESHOLD, (3) post-pivot residual ‖B·B⁻¹eₖ - eₖ‖ > REFACT_RESID_TOL. The check is not confined to Phase 9's primal revised-simplex loop: Phase 11's dual-simplex driver and warm-start path must each call the same gate after applying a ModelDelta or loading a SavedBasis, since a factorization that was fresh when serialized may already be stale relative to a perturbed problem.
  • Tradeoff. Checking freshness after every pivot (rather than on a fixed period, e.g. every 100 pivots) costs one condition estimate and one residual check per pivot — O(nnz(B)) — instead of being free; the alternative (checking only periodically) is exactly the failure mode this principle forbids, because a single badly-conditioned pivot between checks can already corrupt subsequent FTRAN/BTRAN results.
  • Operational. LuFactorization::stats() exposes factorize_count, update_count, and last_cond_estimate (Phase 10); lp-cli --verbose prints them so a stale-but-undetected factorization is visible to an operator even outside a debug build.
  • Violation consequence. A factorization that is refactorized only in lp-revised but not re-validated after lp-dual::warm_start applies a ModelDelta drifts silently: FTRAN/BTRAN solve against the wrong triangular factors, reduced costs and ratio-test ratios are computed from a matrix that no longer corresponds to the actual basis, and the solver selects a pivot that violates I1 (basis nonsingularity) without any single individual computation raising an error.
  • Example of violation. Phase 11 warm-starts from a SavedBasis whose factor_snapshot was computed against a problem before a 5% RHS perturbation; warm_start() skips refactorization because n_updates == 0 looks fresh by the update-count trigger alone, but the residual trigger (‖Bx_B - b‖) — which would have caught the mismatch — was never evaluated for a loaded (as opposed to locally pivoted) factorization; the dual simplex proceeds on a basis that is nonsingular in principle but numerically stale, producing a wrong reduced-cost sign that only the KKT verifier catches at the very end.

7. System Invariants

Invariant Expansion Table

The following twelve invariants define the correctness contract of the solver. Each is expanded with: statement, why it matters, enforcing crate/type, detection mechanism, telemetry emitted, recovery strategy, and debugging methodology.

I1 — Basis Nonsingularity

Field Detail
Statement Basis.factor is a valid non-singular LU of the current basis columns B at all times; any pivot maintains this property
Why it matters All FTRAN/BTRAN triangular solves produce garbage if B is singular; reduced costs, ratio tests, and certificates are all downstream of the basis
Enforcing crate / type lp-lu (LuFactorization); lp-revised checks condition before each FTRAN; lp-simplex checks after each pivot
Detection Condition estimate > REFACT_COND_THRESHOLD; pivot element < PIVOT_TOL post-FTRAN; residual ‖B·B⁻¹eₖ - eₖ‖∞ > REFACT_RESID_TOL in debug mode
Telemetry warn!(condition_est, n_updates, "refactorizing basis — condition threshold exceeded")
Recovery Trigger lp-lu::refactorize(B_cols) from current basis columns; if refactorization itself reveals rank deficiency, return Status::Numerical
Debug method Extract the current basis column matrix; compute B·B⁻¹ explicitly using lp-tableau oracle on a small instance; compare against identity

I2 — Primal Feasibility (Primal Simplex)

Field Detail
Statement During primal simplex Phase II, every basic variable satisfies lⱼ - FEAS_TOL ≤ xⱼ ≤ uⱼ + FEAS_TOL after each pivot
Why it matters Phase II validity rests on maintaining feasibility throughout; an infeasible basic solution means the ratio test selected the wrong leaving variable
Enforcing crate / type lp-bounds (ratio test), lp-simplex (post-pivot assertion in debug builds via check_primal_feas)
Detection max_j ‖[xⱼ - uⱼ]₊ + [lⱼ - xⱼ]₊‖ in debug builds; assert after every pivot when --debug-feas flag is set
Telemetry error!(var_id = j, violation = xⱼ - uⱼ, "primal feasibility violated post-pivot")
Recovery Refactorize basis; re-run ratio test; if violation persists across two consecutive pivots, return Status::Numerical
Debug method Enable --debug-feas; print candidate leaving variables and their step sizes; confirm minimum-ratio selection identifies the correct leaving variable

I3 — Dual Feasibility (Dual Simplex)

Field Detail
Statement During dual simplex, all reduced costs maintain their optimal sign: rcⱼ ≥ -OPT_TOL for NonbasicLower, rcⱼ ≤ OPT_TOL for NonbasicUpper
Why it matters Dual simplex validity rests on dual feasibility; a sign-violated reduced cost means a primal-improving direction exists, contradicting the dual-feasible starting assumption
Enforcing crate / type lp-dual (pricing loop), lp-dual (post-pivot BTRAN check in debug builds)
Detection all_correct_sign(rc, var_statuses, OPT_TOL) checked before each dual pivot; assertion in debug builds
Telemetry error!(var_id = j, rc = rcⱼ, expected_sign, "dual feasibility violated post-pivot")
Recovery Re-price all reduced costs from scratch via BTRAN; if dual feasibility cannot be restored, fall back to cold-start primal simplex
Debug method Verify BTRAN output y = cB·B⁻¹ against explicit matrix multiplication y = cB · inv(B) on a small extracted basis

I4 — Optimality Condition

Field Detail
Statement At Status::Optimal, all nonbasic reduced costs have the correct sign within OPT_TOL: rcⱼ ≥ -OPT_TOL for NonbasicLower, rcⱼ ≤ OPT_TOL for NonbasicUpper, `
Why it matters The optimality stopping criterion; if any rcⱼ has the wrong sign, a strictly better vertex exists and the solver stopped prematurely
Enforcing crate / type lp-simplex (optimality check at exit loop), KKT verifier before constructing Certificate::KktOptimal
Detection KKT verifier checks all reduced costs; max_wrong_sign_rc > OPT_TOL → Status::Numerical instead of Status::Optimal
Telemetry info!(iterations, obj, n_pivots, "optimality certified") on success; error!(worst_rc, var_id, "optimality check failed") on failure
Recovery Not recoverable at runtime — incorrect stopping is a solver logic bug; log violations and return Status::Numerical
Debug method Print all reduced costs at termination; identify the most-negative (for minimize); trace back to which pricing call accepted it as non-improving

I5 — Strong Duality

Field Detail
Statement At Status::Optimal: `
Why it matters Confirms primal and dual solutions are mutually consistent; a duality gap indicates either the dual values or the primal solution is wrong
Enforcing crate / type lp-simplex (post-solve check), lp-sensitivity (dual values from BTRAN at optimality)
Detection Computed in KKT verifier: `gap =
Telemetry warn!(primal_obj, dual_obj, gap, scale, "strong duality gap nonzero")
Recovery Investigate whether BTRAN-computed y is correct; re-run BTRAN; if gap persists after refactorization, return Status::Numerical
Debug method Compare y from lp-sensitivity against explicit B⁻ᵀ·cB on a small known instance; check individual dual variable values

I6 — Ratio-Test Correctness

Field Detail
Statement The leaving variable from the ratio test yields the maximum feasible step; no basic variable is driven outside [lⱼ - FEAS_TOL, uⱼ + FEAS_TOL] after the pivot
Why it matters Selecting too-large a step drives a basic variable out of bounds (primal infeasibility); too-small a step causes unnecessary degeneracy
Enforcing crate / type lp-bounds (ratio_test_bounded()); lp-simplex and lp-dual consume RatioTestResult
Detection Post-pivot assertion: check_primal_feas(x_new, bounds, FEAS_TOL) after pivot; compare step against all candidate ratios in debug mode
Telemetry debug!(entering = j, leaving = k, step, bound_flip, "ratio test result")
Recovery Refactorize and re-run ratio test; if inconsistency persists after refactorization, return Status::Numerical
Debug method Enumerate all candidate leaving variables manually; confirm minimum-ratio selection; test bound-flip condition separately with a unit test

I7 — Finite Termination

Field Detail
Statement The pivot sequence is finite; no basis is revisited indefinitely under any anti-cycling rule
Why it matters Without anti-cycling, degenerate LPs (Beale's example, degenerate network flows) cause infinite loops; the solver must always terminate
Enforcing crate / type lp-pricing (PivotRule::Bland — smallest index), lp-simplex (stall counter, rule switch, iteration limit)
Detection degenerate_count ≥ STALL_DETECT_WINDOW → switch to Bland; iteration_count ≥ MAX_ITER → Status::IterationLimit
Telemetry warn!(iter, degenerate_count, "stall detected, switching to Bland rule")
Recovery Switch to Bland rule; if IterationLimit hit, return partial solution with Status::IterationLimit; increase MAX_ITER for user override
Debug method Run Beale 6-variable cycling LP and Kuhn cycling LP (Phase 14 test suite); confirm termination within 100 pivots with Bland active; verify stall counter triggers

I8 — Certificate Validity

Field Detail
Statement PrimalRay(d): c·d < -OPT_TOL, Ad = 0, d ≥ 0; DualRay(y): yᵀb < -OPT_TOL, Aᵀy ≥ -OPT_TOL component-wise
Why it matters An unverifiable certificate is a meaningless claim; callers rely on the ray as a mathematical proof of status; a wrong ray means incorrect problem classification
Enforcing crate / type lp-simplex (primal ray on unbounded); lp-dual / lp-simplex Phase I (dual ray on infeasible); lp-cli check (external verifier)
Detection verify_certificate(cert, problem, tol) in lp-cli check subcommand and Phase 14 test suite; each condition checked independently
Telemetry info!(cert_type, ‌"certificate verified") or error!(cert_type, cond_violated, "certificate INVALID")
Recovery Re-extract ray from basis columns; if invalid after re-extraction, return Status::Numerical with no certificate
Debug method Manual dot-product check on all certificate conditions for small failing instances; compare primal ray direction against the unbounded entering column from FTRAN

I9 — Presolve/Postsolve Fidelity

Field Detail
Statement The optimal objective of the presolved problem maps to a feasible, equal-objective solution of the original after postsolve: `
Why it matters Presolve must not alter the LP's optimum; wrong postsolve produces an infeasible or suboptimal original solution without detection
Enforcing crate / type lp-presolve (postsolve(sol, stack)), lp-cli (original-space feasibility check at pipeline end)
Detection `
Telemetry info!(reductions = n, rows_removed, cols_fixed, bounds_tightened, "presolve complete")
Recovery Bisect PostsolveStack to identify the first incorrect PostsolveOp; disable that reduction class and re-solve; report as a correctness bug
Debug method Step through PostsolveStack in reverse; after each op, verify primal feasibility of the partially-recovered solution against the partially-restored constraints

I10 — Standard-Form Equivalence

Field Detail
Statement The standard-form problem from lp-model::to_standard_form() has the same optimal objective (after Maximize → negate) and var_map covers all original variables surjectively; A_std x_std = b at any standard-form solution
Why it matters Standard-form conversion is the solver's entry point; a wrong conversion means solving a different problem; var_map must allow exact recovery of original-variable values
Enforcing crate / type lp-model (to_standard_form(), StandardForm.var_map), tested exhaustively in Phase 2 unit tests
Detection Unit tests compare original and standard-form objectives on hand-checked instances; var_map.len() == n_struct assertion; residual ‖A_std x_std - b‖ ≤ FEAS_TOL at any feasible point
Telemetry debug!(n_struct, n_slack, n_artificial, sense, "standard form constructed")
Recovery Standard-form conversion bugs are correctness failures, not runtime-recoverable; caught entirely by Phase 2 unit tests; no runtime recovery path
Debug method Hand-check a 2-variable, 2-constraint LP with known analytic optimum; verify c_std · x_std = c_orig · x_orig and A_std x_std = b; inspect var_map manually

I11 — Basis Nonsingularity at Pivot Selection

Field Detail
Statement No pivot is executed if the resulting basis matrix B would be singular within PIVOT_TOL: the ratio test / pivot-column selection in lp-bounds and lp-lu's Markowitz threshold reject any candidate pivot element `
Why it matters I1 maintains nonsingularity as a state invariant via periodic refactorization; I11 is the entry-time gate that stops a single bad pivot from ever being applied — without it, one degenerate/near-singular pivot between refactorizations can corrupt the basis before any condition-number check fires
Enforcing crate / type lp-lu (Markowitz pivot-acceptance threshold in factor.rs and update.rs), lp-bounds (ratio_test_bounded() rejects a candidate leaving row whose pivot element is below PIVOT_TOL)
Detection Candidate pivot element `
Telemetry warn!(row, col, pivot_val, threshold, "pivot rejected — below PIVOT_TOL")
Recovery Select the next-best candidate column/row (Markowitz ordering already ranks alternatives); if no acceptable pivot exists in the current column, treat as a stability failure and trigger lp-lu::refactorize before retrying
Debug method Log the full ranked candidate list (Markowitz cost + magnitude) for the rejected column; confirm the accepted alternative satisfies PIVOT_TOL on a small hand-traced instance

I12 — Optimality Certificate (KKT Bundle)

Field Detail
Statement At termination with Status::Optimal, the primal/dual pair simultaneously satisfies primal feasibility, dual feasibility, and complementary slackness — the full KKT bundle — within FEAS_TOL/OPT_TOL, and only this verified bundle authorizes constructing Certificate::KktOptimal; I4 (reduced-cost sign) and I5 (duality gap) are each necessary but individually insufficient substitutes for this combined check
Why it matters A solver could satisfy I4 and I5 independently through compensating errors (e.g., a slightly wrong dual value that still closes the duality gap) while complementary slackness is violated; only the conjoined KKT check is an externally verifiable proof of optimality, per P1
Enforcing crate / type lp-core::kkt (verify_kkt(problem, solution, tolerances) -> KktReport), invoked by lp-simplex and lp-revised before returning any Optimal status, and by lp-cli check as an external verifier
Detection KktReport { primal_viol, dual_viol, cs_viol }; any field exceeding its tolerance flips the returned status from Optimal to Numerical before the caller ever sees it
Telemetry info!(primal_viol, dual_viol, cs_viol, "KKT certificate verified") on success; error!(primal_viol, dual_viol, cs_viol, "KKT bundle failed — downgrading to Numerical") on failure
Recovery Re-derive y via BTRAN and recompute reduced costs; if the bundle still fails after recomputation, return Status::Numerical with no certificate rather than a false Optimal
Debug method Run tests/netlib_conformance.rs's KKT verifier (Phase 14) in isolation on the failing instance; inspect which of the three sub-checks (primal/dual/CS) fails first to localize whether the defect is in the ratio test, the pricing/BTRAN path, or the certificate assembly itself

8. Domain Model

8.1 Core Object Taxonomy

Object Module Lifecycle Owned By
LpProblem lp-model created by builder or parser; immutable after seal() caller
StandardForm lp-model derived from LpProblem; recreated on presolve changes solver
Basis lp-revised / lp-tableau mutable per pivot; serialisable for warm-start solver
LuFactorization lp-lu rebuilt periodically; holds eta vectors + pivot permutations Basis
Solution lp-core produced at termination; immutable caller
Certificate lp-core produced with Solution; required field, never None at termination Solution
Sensitivity lp-sensitivity computed post-optimal on demand from dual prices + basis caller
PresolveState lp-presolve stack of undo records; consumed during postsolve solver
ScalingFactors lp-presolve row/column multipliers; applied before solve, removed at postsolve solver

8.2 Variable Status State Machine

Each structural variable (and later each slack/artificial) carries a VarStatus that evolves through the simplex iterations.

              add to basis                  pivot out (ratio test)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   ┌──────────────┐   enter basis   ┌─────────┐                 │
  │   │NonbasicLower │ ─────────────▶  │  Basic  │                 │
  │   └──────────────┘ ◀─────────────  └─────────┘ ──▶ NonbasicUpper
  │         ▲           leave, at ub       │               │
  │         │                              │  leave, at lb │
  │         └──────────────────────────────┘               │
  │                                                         │
  │   ┌──────────────┐                                     │
  │   │  Free (super │ ◀── bound flip during bounded ratio test
  │   │  nonbasic)   │
  └───┴──────────────┘

State-transition table:

From Trigger To Guard
NonbasicLower entering variable selected by pricing Basic reduced cost < -eps_opt (minimise)
NonbasicUpper entering variable selected by pricing Basic reduced cost > +eps_opt
Basic ratio test selects this as leaving at lower bound NonbasicLower x_B[i] - theta * a_ij = lb_i
Basic ratio test selects this as leaving at upper bound NonbasicUpper x_B[i] - theta * a_ij = ub_i
NonbasicLower entering column hits its upper bound before any basic var NonbasicUpper bound flip: theta = ub_j - lb_j
NonbasicUpper entering column hits its lower bound before any basic var NonbasicLower bound flip (decreasing direction)
NonbasicLower/Upper variable fixed by presolve Fixed (removed) lb == ub within eps_feas

8.3 Solver Phase State Machine

  [Parse / Build]
        │
        ▼
  [Presolve + Scale]
        │ problem trivially infeasible? ──▶ Status::Infeasible + DualRay
        ▼
  [Standard Form Conversion]
        │
        ▼
  [Phase I — minimise sum(artificials)]
        │ optimal value > eps_feas ──────▶ Status::Infeasible + DualRay
        │ optimal value <= eps_feas
        ▼
  [Drop artificials; construct primal-feasible basis]
        │
        ▼
  [Phase II — primal or revised simplex loop]
        │ all reduced costs >= -eps_opt ─▶ Status::Optimal ──▶ KKT check
        │ no leaving var on unbounded col ▶ Status::Unbounded + PrimalRay
        │ iter >= iter_limit ─────────────▶ Status::IterationLimit
        │ severe numerical failure ───────▶ Status::Numerical → refactorize / retry
        ▼
  [Postsolve + Unscale]
        │
        ▼
  [Sensitivity Analysis (optional)]
        │
        ▼
  [Return Solution + Certificate]

8.4 LpProblem Memory Layout

LpProblem stores the constraint matrix in column-major CSC (Compressed Sparse Column):

  SparseMatrix {
    nrows : usize,        // m — number of constraints
    ncols : usize,        // n_struct — number of structural vars
    values  : Vec<f64>,   // non-zeros, column order
    row_ind : Vec<usize>, // row index per non-zero
    col_ptr : Vec<usize>, // col_ptr[j]..col_ptr[j+1] is column j's range; len = ncols+1
  }

Column j occupies values[col_ptr[j]..col_ptr[j+1]]. Access row i of column j requires a linear scan of row_ind[col_ptr[j]..col_ptr[j+1]]; this is acceptable because column operations dominate in simplex.

8.5 StandardForm Layout

After lp-model::to_standard_form:

  StandardForm {
    A_std      : SparseMatrix,   // m x (n_struct + n_slack + n_artificial)
    b          : Vec<f64>,       // m — RHS (always >= 0 after row sign flip)
